// Rebuilds the knowledge base from its export: a few text files `base-<n>.txt`, pasted by hand, and
// `library-<n>.txt` when the export carries official documentation (compressed, in base64 lines).
// Run with Node 22.18 or later, on Linux, macOS or Windows, no build step, no package:
//   node import.ts                    reads ./base-*.txt and ./library-*.txt, rebuilds the base here
//   node import.ts --from <folder>    reads the export files from <folder>
//   node import.ts --to <folder>      writes the base into <folder>
//   node import.ts --project          writes only what a project needs: AGENTS.md, .agents/, docs/,
//                                     templates/ at the root, scripts/check-base.ts,
//                                     scripts/check-versions.ts, adapters/opencode/, adapters/mcp/
//                                     and library/
//   node import.ts --opencode 2       also writes opencode.json at the root, from
//                                     adapters/opencode/v2/ (1 for OpenCode 1.x)
//   node import.ts --force            replaces files that already exist and differ
// It checks every part and every file with its SHA-256 checksum, and writes nothing if one fails.
import { createHash } from 'node:crypto';
import {
  existsSync,
  mkdirSync,
  readdirSync,
  readFileSync,
  realpathSync,
  writeFileSync,
} from 'node:fs';
import {
  dirname,
  isAbsolute,
  join,
  normalize,
  resolve,
  sep,
} from 'node:path';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
import { parseArgs } from 'node:util';
import { gunzipSync } from 'node:zlib';

export interface ExportedFile {
  path: string;
  content: string;
}

export interface Part {
  name: string;
  text: string;
}

interface Chunk {
  path: string;
  index: number;
  count: number;
  hash: string;
  content: string;
}

interface ParsedPart {
  number: number;
  total: number;
  chunks: Chunk[];
}

export const FORMAT = 'BASE-EXPORT-1';
export const PART_PATTERN = /^base-\d+\.txt$/;
export const PROJECT_RULES = 'AGENTS.project.md';
export const LIBRARY_FORMAT = 'LIBRARY-EXPORT-1';
export const LIBRARY_PART_PATTERN = /^library-\d+\.txt$/;
const PROJECT_FOLDERS = [
  'code/',
  'scripts/check-base.ts',
  'scripts/check-versions.ts',
  'adapters/opencode/',
  'adapters/mcp/',
  'library/',
];
const OPENCODE_VERSIONS = new Set([
  '1',
  '2',
]);
const NEWLINE = '\n';

export class ImportError extends Error {}

export function sha256(text: string): string {
  return createHash('sha256')
    .update(text, 'utf8')
    .digest('hex');
}

function headerOf(text: string, name: string): {
  marker: string;
  number: number;
  total: number;
  hash: string;
} {
  const match = new RegExp(`^(=+${FORMAT}-[\\da-f]+=+) PART (\\d+) OF (\\d+) SHA256 ([\\da-f]{64})$`).exec(text.slice(0, text.indexOf(NEWLINE)));

  if (match === null) {
    throw new ImportError(`${name}: the first line is not the header of an export part. Paste the whole file again.`);
  }
  return {
    marker: match[1],
    number: Number(match[2]),
    total: Number(match[3]),
    hash: match[4],
  };
}

function chunkHeader(line: string, marker: string, name: string): Omit<Chunk, 'content'> & { addedNewline: boolean } {
  const match = /^FILE (\d+)\/(\d+) ([\da-f]{64}) (NL|RAW) (.+)$/.exec(line.slice(marker.length + 1));

  if (match === null || !line.startsWith(`${marker} `)) {
    throw new ImportError(`${name}: a file header is damaged: ${line}`);
  }
  return {
    index: Number(match[1]),
    count: Number(match[2]),
    hash: match[3],
    addedNewline: match[4] === 'NL',
    path: match[5],
  };
}

export function parsePart(raw: string, name: string): ParsedPart {
  const text = raw
    .replaceAll('\r\n', NEWLINE)
    .replace(/\n*$/, NEWLINE);
  const header = headerOf(text, name);
  const body = text.slice(text.indexOf(NEWLINE) + 1);

  if (sha256(body) !== header.hash) {
    throw new ImportError(`${name}: part ${header.number} of ${header.total} was damaged by the copy. Paste it again, whole.`);
  }
  const lines = body.split(NEWLINE);
  const chunks: Chunk[] = [];

  for (let index = 0; index < lines.length - 1; index += 1) {
    const chunk = chunkHeader(lines[index], header.marker, name);
    const end = lines.indexOf(`${header.marker} END`, index + 1);

    if (end < 0) {
      throw new ImportError(`${name}: the end of ${chunk.path} is missing.`);
    }
    const content = lines
      .slice(index + 1, end)
      .map((line) => line + NEWLINE)
      .join('');

    chunks.push({
      ...chunk,
      content: chunk.addedNewline ? content.slice(0, -1) : content,
    });
    index = end;
  }
  return {
    number: header.number,
    total: header.total,
    chunks,
  };
}

function checkParts(parts: ParsedPart[]): void {
  const total = parts[0]?.total ?? 0;
  const numbers = new Set(parts.map((part) => part.number));
  const missing = Array
    .from({ length: total }, (_, index) => index + 1)
    .filter((number) => !numbers.has(number));

  if (parts.length === 0) {
    throw new ImportError('No export part found: paste the files base-1.txt, base-2.txt… next to import.ts.');
  }
  if (parts.some((part) => part.total !== total)) {
    throw new ImportError('The parts come from two different exports. Delete them all and paste the parts of one export.');
  }
  if (missing.length > 0) {
    throw new ImportError(`Missing parts: ${missing.map((number) => `base-${number}.txt`).join(', ')}.`);
  }
  if (numbers.size !== parts.length) {
    throw new ImportError('A part is there twice. Keep one copy of each part.');
  }
}

function safePath(path: string): string {
  const clean = normalize(path);

  if (isAbsolute(path) || clean
    .split(/[\\/]/)
    .includes('..')) {
    throw new ImportError(`Unsafe path in the export, refused: ${path}`);
  }
  return clean;
}

export function assemble(parts: ParsedPart[]): ExportedFile[] {
  checkParts(parts);
  const byPath = new Map<string, Chunk[]>();

  for (const chunk of parts
    .toSorted((first, second) => first.number - second.number)
    .flatMap((part) => part.chunks)) {
    byPath.set(chunk.path, [
      ...(byPath.get(chunk.path) ?? []),
      chunk,
    ]);
  }
  return [...byPath].map(([
    path,
    chunks,
  ]) => {
    const content = chunks
      .toSorted((first, second) => first.index - second.index)
      .map((chunk) => chunk.content)
      .join('');

    if (chunks.length !== chunks[0].count || sha256(content) !== chunks[0].hash) {
      throw new ImportError(`${path}: incomplete or damaged. Paste its parts again.`);
    }
    return {
      path: safePath(path),
      content,
    };
  });
}

export function forProject(files: ExportedFile[]): ExportedFile[] {
  return files
    .filter((file) => PROJECT_FOLDERS.some((prefix) => file.path.split(sep).join('/').startsWith(prefix)))
    .map((file) => {
      const path = file.path
        .split(sep)
        .join('/');

      return {
        path: path.startsWith('code/') ? path.slice('code/'.length) : path,
        content: file.content,
      };
    });
}

export function writeFiles(files: ExportedFile[], target: string, force: boolean): string[] {
  const conflicts = files.filter((file) => {
    const path = join(target, file.path);

    return existsSync(path) && readFileSync(path, 'utf8') !== file.content;
  });

  if (conflicts.length > 0 && !force) {
    throw new ImportError(`These files exist and differ; nothing was written. Use --force to replace them:\n${conflicts
      .map((file) => `- ${file.path}`)
      .join(NEWLINE)}`);
  }
  for (const file of files) {
    mkdirSync(dirname(join(target, file.path)), { recursive: true });
    writeFileSync(join(target, file.path), file.content);
  }
  return files.map((file) => file.path);
}

// Adds opencode.json at the root, copied from the adapter of the given major version of OpenCode.
export function withOpenCode(files: ExportedFile[], version?: string): ExportedFile[] {
  if (version === undefined) {
    return files;
  }
  if (!OPENCODE_VERSIONS.has(version)) {
    throw new ImportError(`--opencode takes 1 or 2, the major version of OpenCode, not "${version}".`);
  }
  const adapter = `adapters/opencode/v${version}/opencode.json`;
  const source = files.find((file) => file.path.split(sep).join('/') === adapter);

  if (source === undefined) {
    throw new ImportError(`${adapter} is not in the export.`);
  }
  return [
    ...files,
    {
      path: 'opencode.json',
      content: source.content,
    },
  ];
}

export function readParts(folder: string): Part[] {
  return readdirSync(folder)
    .filter((name) => PART_PATTERN.test(name) || LIBRARY_PART_PATTERN.test(name))
    .map((name) => {
      return {
        name,
        text: readFileSync(join(folder, name), 'utf8'),
      };
    });
}

function bytesHash(data: Buffer): string {
  return createHash('sha256')
    .update(data)
    .digest('hex');
}

interface LibraryPart {
  name: string;
  number: number;
  total: number;
  whole: string;
  data: string;
}

function parseLibraryPart(part: Part): LibraryPart {
  const lines = part.text
    .replaceAll('\r', '')
    .split(NEWLINE);
  const match = new RegExp(`^=+${LIBRARY_FORMAT} PART (\\d+) OF (\\d+) SHA256 ([\\da-f]{64}) TOTAL ([\\da-f]{64})=+$`).exec((lines[0] ?? '').trim());

  if (match === null) {
    throw new ImportError(`${part.name}: the first line is not the header of a library part. Paste the whole file again.`);
  }
  const data = lines
    .slice(1)
    .join('')
    .replaceAll(/\s+/g, '');

  if (sha256(data) !== match[3]) {
    throw new ImportError(`${part.name}: library part ${match[1]} of ${match[2]} was damaged by the copy. Paste it again, whole.`);
  }
  return {
    name: part.name,
    number: Number(match[1]),
    total: Number(match[2]),
    whole: match[4] ?? '',
    data,
  };
}

// The library parts: base64 lines of the gzip of one export of the files of library/.
export function decodeLibrary(parts: Part[]): ExportedFile[] {
  if (parts.length === 0) {
    return [];
  }
  const parsed = parts
    .map(parseLibraryPart)
    .toSorted((first, second) => first.number - second.number);
  const total = parsed[0]?.total ?? 0;
  const missing = Array
    .from({ length: total }, (_, index) => index + 1)
    .filter((number) => !parsed.some((part) => part.number === number));

  if (missing.length > 0 || parsed.length !== total || parsed.some((part) => part.total !== total || part.whole !== parsed[0]?.whole)) {
    throw new ImportError(`Library parts missing or from two exports: ${missing.map((number) => `library-${number}.txt`).join(', ') || 'check the numbers'}.`);
  }
  const gzip = Buffer.from(
    parsed
      .map((part) => part.data)
      .join(''),
    'base64',
  );

  if (bytesHash(gzip) !== parsed[0]?.whole) {
    throw new ImportError('The library parts do not fit together. Paste them all again.');
  }
  const text = gunzipSync(gzip).toString('utf8');

  return assemble([parsePart(text, 'library')]);
}

export function importBase(
  parts: Part[],
  target: string,
  options: {
    project: boolean;
    force: boolean;
    opencode?: string;
  },
): string[] {
  const baseParts = parts.filter((part) => !LIBRARY_PART_PATTERN.test(part.name));
  const library = decodeLibrary(parts.filter((part) => LIBRARY_PART_PATTERN.test(part.name)));
  const base = assemble(baseParts.map((part) => parsePart(part.text, part.name)));
  const files = [
    ...base,
    ...library,
  ];
  // The rules of a project are its own: an update of the base never writes AGENTS.project.md.
  const chosen = (options.project ? forProject(files) : files).filter((file) => file.path !== PROJECT_RULES);

  return writeFiles(withOpenCode(chosen, options.opencode), target, options.force);
}

function main(): void {
  const { values } = parseArgs({
    options: {
      from: { type: 'string' },
      to: { type: 'string' },
      project: { type: 'boolean' },
      force: { type: 'boolean' },
      opencode: { type: 'string' },
    },
  });

  try {
    const written = importBase(readParts(resolve(values.from ?? '.')), resolve(values.to ?? '.'), {
      project: values.project === true,
      force: values.force === true,
      ...values.opencode === undefined ? {} : { opencode: values.opencode },
    });

    process.stdout.write(`Import done: ${written.length} files written.\n`);
    if (existsSync(join(resolve(values.to ?? '.'), PROJECT_RULES))) {
      process.stdout.write(`${PROJECT_RULES} kept as it is: it holds the rules of this project.\n`);
    }
  } catch (error) {
    process.stdout.write(`Import failed: ${(error as Error).message}\n`);
    process.exitCode = 1;
  }
}

// realpathSync: on some systems the temp folder is a link, and the two paths differ without it.
if (process.argv[1] !== undefined && realpathSync(process.argv[1]) === realpathSync(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url))) {
  main();
}

# Local AI knowledge base

A shared base of documents, rules, agents and skills that a local AI reads to work better. It has
two profiles, each independent of any software:

- Code: for any AI coding assistant that reads and writes files and runs commands. OpenCode is
  today's target.
- Chat: for any web chat without file access. The AI only answers and explains. Open WebUI is
  today's target.

Status: every topic below is written and checked, version 0.1.0 is ready for its trial on a
workstation (`trials/README.md`).

- Foundation: `code/AGENTS.md` with a map of topics, the five authoring skills, the document
  template, the French summaries and the base check.
- Heavy files: the skill `read-heavy-file` and its script `extract.ts`.
- Topics: lint and analysis for TypeScript, Angular and C#; versions of Angular, TypeScript and
  .NET; containers and Kubernetes; synoptics and equipment catalogs; simulation; architecture;
  reading C, C++, Java and Rust.
- Skills: Git skills, three agents written as skills, a commit assistant for the chat.
- Delivery: the export and the import carry the base by copy and paste; the adapters plug it into
  OpenCode and Open WebUI; a docs server waits for its trial.

This file lists every topic the base covers, in the order they were built.

## Carry the base to a workstation

On a machine that has this repository, with Node 22.18 or later:

```sh
node scripts/export.ts               # writes export/import.ts and export/base-1.txt, base-2.txt…
node scripts/export.ts --max-kb 100  # parts of 100 KB at most instead of 500
node scripts/export.ts --trial       # also the trial guides, samples and evaluation (trials/)
node scripts/export.ts --library core  # also the core set of library/, as compressed parts
```

`--library` takes `core`, `all` or a list of tools (`angular,dotnet-csharp`). Its parts,
`library-<n>.txt`, are gzip then base64 in lines of 76 characters: ASCII only, about 2.6 times less
text to paste than plain Markdown. The export says how many parts each tool adds. The `export/`
folder of this repository holds the export of the last delivery, built with `--trial --library core`.

On the workstation, in an empty folder:

1. Create `import.ts` and paste the content of `export/import.ts` into it.
2. Create `base-1.txt`, `base-2.txt`… and `library-1.txt`… when the export has them, and paste
   each part into the file of the same name.
3. Run `node import.ts`. It checks every part, says which one was damaged by the copy, and writes
   nothing until all of them are right.

`node import.ts --project` writes only what a project needs at its root: `AGENTS.md`, `.agents/`,
`docs/`, `templates/`, `scripts/check-base.ts`, `scripts/check-versions.ts`, `adapters/opencode/`
and `adapters/mcp/`. `--opencode 1` or `--opencode 2` also writes `opencode.json` at the root, for
that major version of OpenCode. `--from` and `--to` change the folders read and written; `--force`
replaces files that already exist and differ. The rules of a project go in `AGENTS.project.md` at
its root (template in `code/templates/`): the import never writes it, so an update of the base keeps
them.

## Check the base

With Node 22.18 or later, from the root of this repository, with no package to install:

```sh
node scripts/check-base.ts          # checks code/, chat/models/ and a generated chat profile
node --test 'scripts/*.test.ts'     # tests of the check, extract.ts, export and import
node scripts/fixtures.ts samples    # fake sample files, to try extract.ts by hand
node scripts/check-library.ts       # the official documentation library (library/)
```

`node scripts/fetch-library.ts` rebuilds `library/` from the documentation repositories listed in
`library/sources.json`, each at a pinned version. It needs git and network access.

The check fails on any finding: `AGENTS.md` too long, a skill without its trigger line or with an
unmarked action step, a document without its header or sources, a request to use the web, French
text in a file for the AI.

## Layout

```text
code/                        Code profile, the single source
  AGENTS.md                  Entry file: short rules, check commands, skill triggers, index
  .agents/skills/<name>/     One skill per folder
    SKILL.md                 The skill; action steps marked for the chat variant
    chat.md                  Optional: chat text that replaces a marked block
    references/              Optional: one detailed document per case, read on demand
    scripts/                 Optional: TypeScript scripts run with node, no package
  docs/                      Reference documents
    README.fr.md             For people: the structure and contents of the whole base, in French
    <topic>/
      README.fr.md           For people: what the topic holds, each file in one line, in French
      <document>.md          One document, one question
  templates/<language>/      Code templates to copy and adapt
chat/                        Chat profile
  models/<model>.md          System prompt of each custom chat model: 4 subjects, commit assistant
  skills/, knowledge/        Generated by the export, never written by hand
adapters/                    Optional files that one software needs
  opencode/v1/, v2/          opencode.json for OpenCode 1.x and 2.x: heavy files refused to the
                             read tool, the three agents, the docs server (off)
  mcp/docs-server.ts         Docs server, one file, no package: search_docs and read_doc; on trial
  open-webui/                push.ts and knowledge-tools.md; manifest.json generated at export
scripts/
  check-base.ts              The base check, copied with the base into a project
  fixtures.ts                Fake sample files for the tests, never copied into a project
  export.ts                  Builds the export and generates the chat profile
  check-public.ts            Checks that this repository can be made public
  evaluation-summary.ts      Sums up an evaluation with and without the base
library/                     Official documentation of the tools, under their own licenses
  sources.json               Each source: repository, pinned version, paths, license, core or not
  <tool>/index.md            One line per file; search it with grep
  <tool>/<page>.md           One page, 16 KB at most, with its source, commit and license
THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md       The license and authors of each source of library/
evaluation/                  The task set and protocol of the evaluation
samples/                     Sample projects for the tests and the trials
trials/                      The trial session on a workstation (README.md), one guide per feature
export/                      The export of the last delivery: import.ts and base-<n>.txt
research/                    Research reports, kept local: its .gitignore keeps them out of Git
import.ts                    Rebuilds the base from its export, one file, no package
```

- Only `code/` is written by hand, plus the chat system prompts in `chat/models/`. The export
  generates the rest of the chat profile from `code/`:
  - `chat/skills/`: each skill without its marked action steps, with a fixed "Limits" block.
  - `chat/knowledge/<topic>/`: the documents of each topic that a chat model names, without the
    French summaries.
- In a project, the contents of `code/` go to the project root, so that the assistant reads
  `AGENTS.md` when it opens the project. `scripts/check-base.ts` goes with them, so that the
  assistant can check what it adds to the base.
- `adapters/` is optional: the base works without it. Changing software means changing or deleting
  one adapter, never the content.
- Open WebUI: after the import on the workstation, `node adapters/open-webui/push.ts` creates or
  updates, through the API of Open WebUI 0.13.3, the knowledge bases, the skills and the models of
  `adapters/open-webui/manifest.json`, readable by one group. It reads `OPEN_WEBUI_URL`,
  `OPEN_WEBUI_TOKEN`, `OPEN_WEBUI_BASE_MODEL`, `OPEN_WEBUI_SHARE` (`group:<name>`, `public` or
  `private`) and `OPEN_WEBUI_MODE` (`native` or `legacy`) from the environment; `--dry-run` sends
  nothing. It keeps the knowledge ids in `state.local.json`, never in Git. With
  `OPEN_WEBUI_LIBRARY` (`core`, `all` or a list of tools), it also pushes one knowledge base per tool
  of `library/`, given to the chat model that `library/sources.json` names for it.
- The AI reads `library/` second: when the documents of the base do not answer, it searches the index
  of one tool with grep, then reads one section of one file (`AGENTS.md`, skill `look-up-api`, and
  the docs server).

## Principles

- Every instruction for the AI is written in English, in short imperative sentences, the vital
  rules first and repeated at the end.
- Little is always loaded: a short `AGENTS.md` with a map of topics, and the list of skills. Each
  topic has its own index, `docs/<topic>/index.md`.
  Documents and skill bodies are read on demand; the AI never reads the whole folder.
- Every choice left to the model gets an explicit trigger: `AGENTS.md` says which skill to load for
  which task.
- What a tool can check (format, lint, types, tests, commit messages) is checked by the tool: the
  base gives the exact command and the loop write, check, fix.
- Every document is self-contained: it holds what the AI needs to know. Links only cite sources.
- Every document built from research cites its sources.
- One source, two variants:
  - Files variant (code profile): the AI writes files and runs commands.
  - Chat variant (chat profile): the AI shows the code and the command to run; it never claims to
    write a file or run a command.
- An agent is a skill. Agent mode, where a software offers it, only adds a short entry in that
  software's adapter.
- Language of the answer: the AI answers in the language of the question, never a mix of two. The
  base itself is in English; the AI reads it in English whatever the language of the question.
- French summaries are for people only: one overview of the whole base and one summary per topic,
  so that the team understands the structure and what each file holds. The AI never reads them
  (`AGENTS.md` says so), and the export leaves them out of the chat profile.

## Order of work

1. Research first, before any topic:
   - How to research a topic and write a document of the base.
   - How to write and organize a knowledge base that a smaller model follows well: instructions,
     skills, agents, documents, loading, checks by tools, evaluation.
   - How to bring the official documentation of each tool into the base, at the version the project
     uses.
2. Then the topics below, priority 1 first.

The research is done: four starting researches and one research per topic. Their reports stay
out of this repository; each topic is built from its report.

## Priority 1

### 1. How the AI uses the base

- Entry file `AGENTS.md`, read when the assistant opens the project root.
- Compact index in `AGENTS.md`: one line per document, with its topic. When it grows past about
  2,000 tokens, it becomes a map of topics with one index per topic.
- Reading rules: open only the documents of the topic; never load the whole base.
- Writing style of every rule, agent and skill: English, short, imperative.

### 2. Skills

- Authoring skills:
  - Write an `AGENTS.md` file.
  - Write a skill.
  - Write an agent.
  - Write a document of the base and its index line, and update the French summary of its topic.
  - Write the system prompt of a custom chat model.
- Git skills:
  - Write a commit message: Conventional Commits, checked by commitlint.
  - Choose a version number: Semantic Versioning.
  - Name a branch: Conventional Branch.
- Topic skills: the skills that support each topic below.

### 3. Writing code: TypeScript and Angular

- Lint rules (ESLint) written as readable rules, each with a wrong and a right example.
- Ready-to-use lint configuration files.
- Strict formatting rules, stricter than Prettier, switched on or off by one setting.
- Reference documentation of TypeScript, Angular and their common APIs.
- Lint loop: after writing code, the AI runs the lint in the dev container and fixes it until zero
  errors. In the chat profile, the AI gives the command to run instead.
- Always TypeScript, never plain JavaScript.

### 4. Writing code: C# and .NET

- Analyzer rules (.NET analyzers) written as readable rules, each with a wrong and a right example.
- Ready-to-use configuration files (`.editorconfig`, analyzer settings).
- Strict formatting rules, switched on or off by one setting.
- Reference documentation of C#, .NET and their common APIs.
- Lint loop in the dev container, until zero errors. In the chat profile, the AI gives the command.

### 5. Containers and deployment

- Docker and Podman.
- Dev containers.
- Kubernetes.
- Deploying containers.

### 6. Synoptics and symbols

- Rules to represent symbols on a synoptic (layout, states, colours, animation).
- Standards the symbols come from (ISO 1219, IEC 60617, ISA 5.1, ISO 7010), cited by reference,
  never copied.
- Symbol catalogue, each symbol with proposed property names in English:
  - Hydraulics.
  - Pneumatics and compressed air.
  - Electrical.
  - Fire safety (detectors, alarms, extinguishing).
  - Fuel, oil and sea water systems.
  - Ventilation and air conditioning.
  - Propulsion and power generation.
  - Sensors and instrumentation (pressure, temperature, level, flow).
- French to English index of symbol and equipment names, for example "vanne → valve".
- Physical equipment (valves, sensors, actuators, pumps…): how it really works, then how it is
  modelled.

### 7. Chat profile

- Skills shared with the whole team, imported without manual edits.
- Knowledge bases shared with the whole team, imported without manual edits.
- Four custom models, one per subject, each with only the skills and knowledge bases of its
  subject:
  - TypeScript and Angular code.
  - C# and .NET code.
  - Containers and Kubernetes.
  - Simulation and naval (synoptics, symbols, vocabulary).
- Each system prompt tells the model to search its knowledge base before it answers.

## Priority 2

### 8. Agents

- .NET and Angular architecture.
- Code review.
- Physical equipment: real operation first, then its model.
- Each agent is a skill first, so that it works where agent mode does not exist.

### 9. Reading file formats

- Never read a binary or heavy file raw: extract its text or structure first, then read only what
  the task needs.
- One way per format, the cleanest available: built into the platform, or an official or
  well-known library; nothing obscure to install.
- Formats: the files a development team exchanges in an office, for example:
  - Office documents, spreadsheets and presentations, with what they embed (sheets, formulas,
    charts, images, comments).
  - PDF, including scanned pages and forms.
  - Design files (Adobe XD and others), images, SVG and other vector files, diagrams.
  - Emails, archives, and large data files (JSON, XML, CSV, logs).
- For each format: what it is made of, the command or script that extracts it, and what to read
  next.

## Priority 3

### 10. Software architecture

- Microservices and APIs.
- .NET backend patterns.
- Angular frontend patterns.
- Contracts between a .NET API and an Angular client (REST, OpenAPI, real-time messages).

### 11. Simulation

- Principles of simulation.
- Architecture of a simulation software.
- Time management: fixed step, variable step, real time.
- Component models: physical equations, states, inputs and outputs.
- Distributed simulation and co-simulation standards (HLA, DIS, FMI).

### 12. Reading code: C, C++, Java and Rust

- One reading guide per language, so that the AI can explain a code excerpt it is given.
- Build files met when reading these projects: CMake, Make, Maven, Gradle, Cargo.

## Delivery and evaluation

### 13. Export and import

- One command builds the export: few text files, 500 KB each at most by default, the limit changed
  by one setting. It generates the chat profile and the adapters.
- Import script: one TypeScript file, run with `node import.ts` on Linux with Node 22.18 or later,
  with no build step and no npm package.
- The import, run on the export in an empty folder, rebuilds the same tree as the base.

### 14. Keeping the base up to date

- Projects update their tool versions once or twice a year; the documents follow.
- Each document records its tool, its version and the date it was checked.
- One simple, written procedure updates the documents of one tool to a new version: find what
  changed between the two versions, rewrite only the sections it touches, check again.
- A check lists the documents whose version is behind the version a project uses.
- Angular comes first: it is the tool the team uses most.

### 15. Evaluation

- A set of test tasks, run with and without the base.
- The AI must do better with the base, without using more tokens.

## License

- Everything written for this base, documents and scripts, is under the MIT License: see
  `LICENSE`.
- The documents of the base summarize official documentation in their own words and cite it.
- `library/` holds official documentation of other projects, each under its own license (CC BY 4.0,
  CC BY-SA 2.5, Apache 2.0, MIT), with its authors in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` and its source in
  the header of each file. The MIT License of the base does not apply to it.

name: analyze-csharp
description: Format C# code, run the .NET analyzers through the build, and fix every reported error until the build is clean. Use when you write or change a C# file, or when the user asks to lint, analyze, format or build C# code.
license: MIT

# Analyze C# code

## Rules

- Fix the code. Never change `.editorconfig`, `Directory.Build.props` or a `.globalconfig` file to pass.
- Never add `#pragma warning disable`, `[SuppressMessage]`, `<NoWarn>` or the `!` operator to hide an error.
- Never write `async void`, except for an event handler.
- Never fix formatting by hand. The format command does it.
- Fix one error ID at a time. Start with the first error of the list.
- Stop after 3 failed attempts on the same error. Show the error to the user.
- Never search the web. Never open a URL.

## Steps

1. Note the C# files you wrote or changed. Follow the "Right" examples of `docs/csharp-analysis/` for the kind of code you write.

2. Check the code against the C# rule documents of the knowledge. Fix what breaks a rule. Show the fixed code.
3. Give the user these commands to run, in this order: `bash scripts/format-csharp.sh`, then `dotnet build --no-restore -tl:off -nologo -v:q`.
4. Ask the user to paste the output lines that contain `error`.
5. For each pasted error: name its ID, give the cause in one sentence, and show the fixed code.
6. Ask the user to run the commands again. Repeat until the user reports zero errors.

7. Give the result: the IDs you fixed, and every error left.

## Example

Wrong:

```csharp
#pragma warning disable CA2016
string text = await File.ReadAllTextAsync(path);
#pragma warning restore CA2016
```

Right:

```csharp
string text = await File.ReadAllTextAsync(path, cancellationToken);
```

## Pitfalls

- Many errors after one change: fix the first one, then build again. Later errors often disappear.
- The base has no section for the ID: read the error message and fix the code as the message says.
- The build fails and step 5 lists nothing: read the end of the log with `tail -n 20 /tmp/build.log`.
- The build says that the assets file is missing: run `dotnet restore` once, then go back to step 2.
- A file changed after the format command: read it again before you edit it.
- The rule looks wrong for this code: do not suppress it. Stop and ask the user.

## Limits

- You cannot read or write files. You cannot run commands.
- When a step needs a command, give the command to the user and ask for its output.
- Never say that you read a file, wrote a file or ran a command.

## Final check

- Did the build end with `exit=0`, and did the format check pass?
- Did you fix the code, with no suppression and no change to the configuration?
- Is there no `async void` outside an event handler?
=====BASE-EXPORT-1-571e463b6744===== END

name: architecture-review
description: Review the architecture of a .NET and Angular application and report risks, each with evidence and the smallest fix. Use when the user asks to review, audit or challenge an architecture, a solution structure, an API contract, a real-time design or a choice of library.
license: MIT

  variant: chat

# Architecture review

## Role

You are a software architect who reviews a .NET backend and an Angular frontend deployed in containers.

## Rules

- Review only. Never change a file.
- Start from the goals of the user. Ask for them if they are missing.
- Judge each choice against a quality goal: performance, availability, changeability, security, cost of operation.
- Prefer the simplest design that meets the goals. Flag every service, layer or library that has no stated reason.
- Give evidence for each risk: a file, a package version or a fact given by the user.
- Give one fix per risk. Give the smallest fix.
- Trust the documents of `docs/architecture/` more than your memory.
- Check each library you name in `docs/architecture/outdated-libraries-and-patterns.md`. If the base does not list it, write "license not checked".
- Never search the web. Never open a URL.

## Steps

1. Ask for what is missing: what the application does, team size, number of deployable units, load figures, quality goals in order of priority.

2. Ask the user to paste: the list of projects with their references, the package lists with versions for .NET and for npm, and the folder tree of each `src/` folder, two levels deep.
3. Write the inventory: deployable units, projects and their references, packages with versions, target frameworks.

4. Search your knowledge for the architecture review checklist. Keep only the sections that match the inventory.
5. For each kept section, search your knowledge for the matching architecture document.

6. For each question you cannot answer from the inventory, ask the user for one file or one excerpt. Ask at most 3 questions per turn.

7. Classify each finding: risk, trade-off or good decision.
8. Sort the risks: first what blocks a quality goal, then license and outdated-library risks, then over-engineering.
9. Write the report with the answer format below.

## Answer format

1. Verdict, in one to three sentences.
2. Risks, at most 10 lines, in a table: `Risk | Evidence | Quality goal hurt | Smallest fix`.
3. Trade-offs to decide, at most 5 lines.
4. Decisions to record, one line each: `Decision | Reason | Alternative rejected`.
5. The documents of the base you used, by title.

## Example

Wrong:

```text
Consider microservices and a mediator library for better scalability.
```

Right:

```text
Risk: 7 services for one team, all released together.
Evidence: one pipeline, one shared database.
Quality goal hurt: changeability.
Smallest fix: merge into one deployable with one module per service. Keep one schema per module.
```

## Pitfalls

- A risk without evidence: name the file, the package version or the fact.
- A split into services with no driver: ask which team, load or release need requires it.
- A library named from memory: check `outdated-libraries-and-patterns.md` first.
- Reading the whole solution: read project files first, then at most 3 files per question.
- A long list of patterns: report at most 10 risks, the most serious first.

## Limits

- You cannot read or write files. You cannot run commands.
- When a step needs a command, give the command to the user and ask for its output.
- Never say that you read a file, wrote a file or ran a command.

## Final check

- Has every risk an evidence and a smallest fix?
- Did you check each library you named against the base?
- Did you change no file?

name: check-container-change
description: Check a changed Dockerfile, devcontainer.json, Kubernetes manifest, Helm chart or kustomization with tools, and fix it until zero errors. Use when you write or change one of these files, or when the user asks to check or validate one.

# Check a container change

## Rules

- Check with a tool. Never say that a file is valid from memory.
- Run only the tools that are installed. Never install a tool.
- Never search the web. Never open a URL.
- Never run a command that changes a cluster or a registry: no `kubectl apply` without `--dry-run`, no `helm install`, no `docker push`.
- Say which checks you could not run.
- Write a new file from the templates of `templates/containers/` and the "Right" examples of `docs/containers/` and `docs/kubernetes/`.

## File types and tools

Use the command of `## Check commands` in `AGENTS.md` when it has one for the file type. It can give the cluster version and the local schema folder. Otherwise use the commands below, in order.

| Type | How to recognize it | Tools |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Image build file | `Dockerfile`, `Containerfile`, `*.Dockerfile` | `hadolint`, `docker` |
| Dev container | `devcontainer.json` | `devcontainer` |
| Kubernetes manifest | YAML file with `apiVersion` and `kind` | `kubeconform`, `kube-linter` |
| Helm chart | Folder with `Chart.yaml` | `helm`, `kubeconform` |
| Kustomization | Folder with `kustomization.yaml` | `kustomize`, `kubeconform` |

Commands, by type:

```sh
# Image build file
hadolint <file>
docker info >/dev/null && docker build --check -f <file> <context folder>
# Dev container
devcontainer read-configuration --workspace-folder <project root>
# Kubernetes manifest
kubeconform -strict -summary <files>
kube-linter lint <files>
# Helm chart
helm lint --strict <chart folder>
helm template <chart folder> | kubeconform -strict -summary
# Kustomization
kustomize build <folder> | kubeconform -strict -summary
```

## Steps

1. Name each file of the task. Give each file one type from the table.

2. Give the user the commands of the type, in one code block.
3. Ask the user to run them and to paste the output.
4. When the user pastes the output, give the corrected file. Say which lines changed and which rule code each change fixes.
5. Ask the user to run the same commands again. Repeat until zero errors.

6. Give the result in the answer format.

## Answer format

1. The result in one sentence: all checks pass, or how many errors are left.
2. One line per file: the file, the tool, the result.
3. `Not checked:` and each tool that was missing or each check that could not run.

## Example

Wrong:

```dockerfile
FROM mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet
COPY publish/ /app
CMD dotnet /app/App.dll
```

`hadolint` prints DL3006 (the image has no tag) and DL3025 (`CMD` is not in JSON form).

Right:

```dockerfile
FROM mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0
WORKDIR /app
COPY publish/ ./
USER $APP_UID
ENTRYPOINT ["dotnet", "App.dll"]
```

## Pitfalls

- `docker build --check` prints `failed to connect to the docker API`: no Docker daemon answers. Skip this check, keep the `hadolint` result, and add it to `Not checked:`.
- `devcontainer read-configuration` runs `docker ps` first. Without a container engine it fails: add it to `Not checked:` and ask the user to rebuild the dev container.
- `kubeconform` fails to download a schema: its schema location is remote. Use the local schema folder of `AGENTS.md`. If there is none, add it to `Not checked:`.
- With Podman, write the full image name with its registry, for example `docker.io/hadolint/hadolint`.
- A file that passes every linter can still fail to build or to start. Say so when no build ran.

## Limits

- You cannot read or write files. You cannot run commands.
- When a step needs a command, give the command to the user and ask for its output.
- Never say that you read a file, wrote a file or ran a command.

## Final check

- Did every result come from a tool that you ran in this session?
- Did you list every check that you could not run?
- Did you avoid every command that changes a cluster or a registry?

=====BASE-EXPORT-1-571e463b6744===== FILE 1/1 621db40f57189737330b2d2c3cbe273c792c54a0ff5295697c92a95d3c8d29a8 RAW chat/skills/choose-version/SKILL.md
---
name: choose-version
description: Choose the next version number with Semantic Versioning 2.0.0, from the commits since the last release. Use when the user asks which version to release, or asks to tag a release.
license: MIT
metadata:
  variant: chat
  version: "0.1.0"
---

# Choose a version number

## Rules

- A version is `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`: three whole numbers, no leading zero.
- Read the commits since the last release. The highest change decides:

  | Commits since the last release | Next version from 1.4.2 |
  | --- | --- |
  | At least one breaking change: `!` before the colon, or a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer | `2.0.0`: MAJOR + 1, MINOR and PATCH back to 0 |
  | Else at least one `feat` | `1.5.0`: MINOR + 1, PATCH back to 0 |
  | Else at least one `fix` or `perf` | `1.4.3`: PATCH + 1 |
  | Only `docs`, `style`, `refactor`, `test`, `build`, `ci` or `chore` | No release needed. If the user wants one: `1.4.3` |

- Before 1.0.0, anything may change. This base uses one rule: a breaking change gives MINOR + 1, everything else PATCH + 1. Say that it is a convention.
- A pre-release adds a hyphen and dot-separated identifiers: `2.0.0-rc.1`. It comes before `2.0.0`.
- Build metadata after a plus sign (`2.0.0+build.7`) does not change the order of versions.
- Never reuse a version that was released. Never go back to a lower version.
- `v1.4.2` is a tag name, not a version. Use the tag form the project already uses.
- Never search the web. Never open a URL.

## Steps

1. Find the last released version and the commits since then. If the user gave them, use them.

2. Ask the user for the last released version and the list of commit headers since then.

3. Mark each commit as breaking, feature, fix or other, with the table above.
4. Take the highest change. Compute the next version from the last one.
5. Give the version, the commit that decided it, and the tag name to create.

6. Give the user the tag name. Never say that you created it.

## Example

Last version `0.3.1`. Commits since then:

```text
feat(alarms): add the alarm history view
fix(pumps): stop the pump when the tank is empty
docs: explain the alarm priorities
```

Wrong: `1.0.0`. Nothing asks for a first stable release.

Right: `0.4.0`, decided by `feat(alarms): add the alarm history view`. Tag: `v0.4.0` if the project tags with a `v`.

## Pitfalls

- A breaking change written only in the body, without `BREAKING CHANGE:`: ask the user before you choose.
- Commits that do not follow Conventional Commits: ask the user what each one changes.
- The version of a package file that differs from the last tag: say it, and ask which one is right.

## Limits

- You cannot read or write files. You cannot run commands.
- When a step needs a command, give the command to the user and ask for its output.
- Never say that you read a file, wrote a file or ran a command.

## Final check

- Did the highest change of all the commits decide the version?
- Did MINOR and PATCH go back to 0 where the table says so?
- Did you create or push nothing that the user did not ask for?

## Sources

- Semantic Versioning 2.0.0, semver.org, CC BY 3.0, summarized.
- Conventional Commits 1.0.0, conventionalcommits.org, CC BY 3.0, summarized.

# Code review

## Role

You are a code reviewer of a .NET and Angular team.

## Rules

- Review only. Never change a file.
- Review only what the lint and the build cannot check. Run the checks that change no file first and report their result in one line.
- Never run `npm run fix` or a formatter: they change files.
- Give the file and the line of each problem, and one fix.
- Mark a personal preference as "optional". It never blocks.
- Say what is well done, in one line.
- Never search the web. Never open a URL.

## Steps

1. Name the files of the change and their languages.

2. Ask the user for the output of the check commands of each language of the change, if it is not given.
3. Search your knowledge for the code review checklist. Keep section A and the sections of the languages of the change.
4. Read the code the user pasted. For each question of the kept sections, note the problems with their line.

5. Sort the problems: first what breaks behavior, then risks, then readability, then optional points.
6. Write the review with the answer format below.

## Answer format

1. Verdict, in one sentence: approve, or change requested.
2. Checks: the result of the check commands, in one line.
3. Problems, at most 10 lines, in a table: `File:line | Problem | Fix`.
4. What is well done, in one line.
5. The documents of the base you used, by title.

## Example

Wrong:

```text
The code looks fine but could be cleaner.
```

Right:

```text
Change requested.
Checks: npm run check, 0 errors.
| src/app/valve/valve-view.ts:42 | The state is changed in place under OnPush: the view does not refresh | Write a new value to the signal |
Well done: one component per symbol.
Code review checklist.
```

## Limits

- You cannot read or write files. You cannot run commands.
- When a step needs a command, give the command to the user and ask for its output.
- Never say that you read a file, wrote a file or ran a command.

## Final check

- Has every problem a file, a line and a fix?
- Did you skip what the lint already checks?
- Did you change no file?
